 \int\sum_w\tau_w(P,Ps)f_w(s)\,d\vartheta_{d,k}(P).$$ Fix $E=B(z,R)$, and let $g_w(P,u)$ be the exact density in Corollary <a href="#p:frame-n30" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:frame-n30">11</a> for $\mathbf 1_Ef_w\sigma$. After integrating <a href="#p:haar-routing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:haar-routing">[p:haar-routing]</a> over $E$, pushforward integration yields $\int\!\int\sum_w\tau_w g_w\,du\,d\vartheta_{d,k}$. Each $g_w$ is supported in $\left\lVert u-Pz\right\rVert\le R$. On this domain the measure with density $\tau_w$, relative to frame probability, label Lebesgue measure, and counting measure in $w$, has total mass at most $v_kR^k$. Hölder’s inequality therefore gives $$\int_E f_{\mathrm{in}}\,d\sigma
 \le (v_kR^k)^{1-1/q}
 \left(\sum_w\int\!\!\int\tau_w g_w^q\,du\,d\vartheta_{d,k}\right)^{1/q}
 \le (v_kR^k)^{1-1/q}N^{1/q}\max_w\left\lVert g_w\right\rVert_{L^q}.$$ Substitute <a href="#p:frame-n30-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:frame-n30-bound">[p:frame-n30-bound]</a>. The radius powers add exactly to $\beta$. Also $k\ge d/8$, so <a href="#p:unit-ball-volume" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:unit-ball-volume">[p:unit-ball-volume]</a> gives $$v_k^{1-1/q}(C\sqrt d)^{k(1-1/q)}\le C_0^d.$$ This proves <a href="#p:haar-block-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:haar-block-bound">[p:haar-block-bound]</a>. The original $Q_w$ could use all entries of $H$; only after that dependence was retained did we average it into $\tau_w$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### Backward propagation of continuation success

We now apply Proposition <a href="#p:haar-block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:haar-block">14</a> to the success probability of a fresh continuation from each state. Its local-mass exponent survives every block, and the resulting finite-dimensional estimate retains the full dependence on $M$.

<a id="stmt-p-haar-success-proposition"></a>
<div id="p:haar-success-proposition" class="proposition">

<a id="p:haar-success"></a>
**Proposition 15** (Success bound from orthonormal projections). *There are absolute constants $C\ge1$ and $d_0$ such that the following holds for every integer $d\ge d_0$, every nonnegative integer $M$, every $0<\epsilon\le1/10$, and every learner of deterministic finite horizon $T$ in Section <a href="#p:geometry-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:geometry-model">2</a>. Put $$k=q=\lfloor d/4\rfloor,\qquad
 b=\lceil T/k\rceil,\qquad N_*=(k+2)2^M.$$ Then $$\label{p:haar-success}
 \mathbb P\{\text{success}\}\le
 C^d\epsilon^q\bigl[C^dN_*^{1/q}\bigr]^b.$$ The constant and dimension threshold are independent of $M,\epsilon,T$ and the learner’s rules.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-p-haar-success-proposition"><summary>Proof of stmt-p-haar-success-proposition</summary>

*Proof.* Use the fixed-seed and Borel-kernel convention of Section <a href="#p:geometry-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:geometry-model">2</a>, and set $n=d-1$ and $\beta=n-q$. For each terminal state and stopping index, let $h(s)$ be the success probability of its signal-independent output law. The cap estimates of Lemma <a href="#p:cap-mass" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:cap-mass">3</a> give $$\int_{B(z,R)}h\,d\sigma
 \le C^d\min\{R^n,\epsilon^n\}
 \le C^d\epsilon^q R^\beta
 \qquad(z\in\mathbb R^d,\ R>0).$$ The last inequality follows from $R\le\epsilon$ or $R\ge\epsilon$, respectively. Thus the all-radii coefficient for every terminal function is at most $K_0=C^d\epsilon^q$.

Divide the horizon into $b$ blocks of $k$ rows, padding the last block with independent rows ignored by its rules. Starting at a specified state and boundary index, define its continuation by a fresh future execution. This definition does not condition on that state being reached. The continuation functions are Borel and take values in $[0,1]$ by Lemma <a href="#p:borel-kernels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:borel-kernels">6</a>.

Within a block, a destination is either a continuing state at the next boundary or a terminal state tagged by its local stopping index. The possible local stopping indices are $0,1,\ldots,k$, including an immediate stop. There are at most $(k+1)2^M$ terminal destinations and $2^M$ continuing destinations, hence at most $N_*$ in total. The global stopping index is determined by the fixed block start and this local index, so the terminal output retains its full allowed dependence on time.

Finite composition of the transition kernels produces Borel routing probabilities $Q_w(X,y)$ for these destinations, with $\sum_wQ_w\le1$. Their continuation functions $f_w$ use only future independent rows and coins; terminal destinations use the functions $h$ above. The incoming continuation therefore has precisely the form $$f_{\mathrm{in}}(s)=\mathbb E_X\sum_wQ_w(X,Xs)f_w(s)$$ from Proposition <a href="#p:haar-block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:haar-block">14</a>. The Gaussian block is fresh: conditional on the fixed shared seed, its rows are jointly independent of the signal and entering state, which depends only on initialization, past pairs, and past coins.

Backward induction through the blocks multiplies the all-radii coefficient by at most $C^dN_*^{1/q}$ per block. This factor is at least one, so the coefficient also bounds any earlier terminal option. Integrate the initial continuation over $B(0,1)$ and average over the conditional initial-state law, which is independent of the signal and rows. Conditional success is at most the right side of <a href="#p:haar-success" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:haar-success">[p:haar-success]</a>. The bound is uniform in the fixed rules, so the shared-seed convention gives the same bound for the original learner. When $T=0$, the terminal bound supplies the conclusion directly. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-p-haar-success-proposition-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-p-haar-success-proposition</summary>

<a id="p:haar-memory-arithmetic"></a>
*Proof of Corollary <a href="#p:finite-memory-tradeoff" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:finite-memory-tradeoff">5</a> and first proof of Theorem <a href="#p:regression" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:regression">4</a>.* Put $L=\log(1/\epsilon)$. Choose an absolute dimension threshold so that Proposition <a href="#p:haar-success-proposition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:haar-success-proposition">15</a> applies and $k=q\ge d/8$. If success is at least $2/3$, <a href="#p:haar-success" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:haar-success">[p:haar-success]</a> implies $$qL\le (b+1)d\log C
       +\frac bq\bigl(M\log2+\log(k+2)\bigr)
       +\log(3/2).$$ Since $b\le8T/d+1$ and $\log(k+2)/q$ is bounded by an absolute constant, this gives an absolute $C_1$ such that $$\label{p:haar-memory-arithmetic}
 dL\le C_1(T+d)\left(1+\frac M{d^2}\right).$$ No bound on $M$ was used. Lemma <a href="#p:haar-linear" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:haar-linear">7</a> forces $T>d/8$, and hence $T+d<9T$. Substitution into <a href="#p:haar-memory-arithmetic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:haar-memory-arithmetic">[p:haar-memory-arithmetic]</a> proves Corollary <a href="#p:finite-memory-tradeoff" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:finite-memory-tradeoff">5</a> with an absolute constant.

Finally, if $M(d)=o(d^2)$, then $1+M(d)/d^2\le2$ for all sufficiently large $d$. The corollary proves Theorem <a href="#p:regression" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:regression">4</a>, with an absolute lower-bound constant and a dimension threshold that may depend on the memory sequence. This proof is uniform over the accuracy sequence and uses only within-block stopping indices. ◻

</details>

<a id="p:cap-section"></a>
### Positive domination by cap measures

This section proves the sample bound by propagating the joint law of the signal and each memory state forward through the stream. We bound each such law by a positive sum of uniform measures on spherical caps. A term is charged for its mass and for a power of the reciprocal of its radius. The main step shows that a block of observations increases the total charge by a controlled factor. The passage from bounds on test functions to an actual dominating measure requires compactness in the norm of $L^1$.

We prove Theorem <a href="#p:regression" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:regression">4</a> by a forward argument in the finite-state experiment of Section <a href="#p:geometry-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:geometry-model">2</a>. Use that section’s fixed-seed convention and Borel kernels from Lemma <a href="#p:borel-kernels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:borel-kernels">6</a>. All bounds below are uniform in the fixed rules; their scalar probability conclusions are averaged over the shared seed at the end.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### The spherical experiment and the projection input

<a id="p:cap-parameters"></a>
Write $\sigma$ for uniform probability on $S^{d-1}$, and set $$\label{p:cap-parameters}
 n=d-1,\qquad k=p=\lfloor d/16\rfloor,\qquad
 \alpha=\lambda=\frac n2,\qquad \theta=1-\frac1p.$$ We take $d$ sufficiently large that $p\ge2$ and $k,p\ge d/32$. For $c\in S^{d-1}$ and $0<r\le2$, define $$B(c,r)=\{s\in S^{d-1}:\left\lVert s-c\right\rVert\le r\},\qquad
 \mu_{c,r}(ds)=\frac{\mathbf 1_{B(c,r)}(s)}{\sigma(B(c,r))}\,\sigma(ds).$$ These are spherical caps and their normalized probability measures. For an ambient ball in $\mathbb R^d$ we use the distinct notation $D(b,t)=\{u\in\mathbb R^d:\left\lVert u-b\right\rVert\le t\}$.

Lemma <a href="#p:cap-mass" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:cap-mass">3</a> supplies the two-sided estimate <a id="p:cap-volume-bound"></a>$$\label{p:cap-volume-bound}
 (ar)^n\le\sigma(B(c,r))\le(A_0r)^n,
 \qquad a=(2\pi)^{-1},\quad A_0=1.$$ In particular every $\mu_{c,r}$ is well defined.

Let $X$ be a $k\times d$ matrix with independent standard Gaussian entries and put $$H=(XX^{\mathsf T})^{1/2},\qquad P=H^{-1}X$$ on the probability-one event that $X$ has full row rank. Define $P$ arbitrarily on the remaining null set. We may make this definition measurable. On the full-rank event $PP^{\mathsf T}=I_k$ and $X=HP$; the law of $P$ is invariant under right orthogonal multiplication. Let $v_k$ be the Lebesgue volume of the unit ball of $\mathbb R^k$ and let $m_k(dz)=v_k^{-1}\mathbf 1_{\{\left\lVert z\right\rVert\le1\}}\,dz$ be uniform probability on that ball.

The use of independent replicas for high projection moments is related to the argument of Sharan, Sidford, and Valiant (Sharan, Sidford, and Valiant 2019, sec. 7, Lemma 12) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[SSV2019](#ref-SSV2019)]. We use the normalized moment theorem (Theorem <a href="#p:frame-positive" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:frame-positive">12</a>) with the parameters in <a href="#p:cap-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:cap-parameters">[p:cap-parameters]</a>. Its inverse-distance margin satisfies $\lambda-(p-2)-k\ge3d/8$. Below we verify its unit support, mass, local-growth, independence, and absolute-continuity hypotheses for each finite positive test measure before invoking its normalized density bound.

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### One block: from bounded tests to a dominating measure

<a id="p:cap-reference-probabilities"></a>
Fix a parent cap $B=B(c,r)$. Let $\mathcal W$ be a finite set and let $K_w(X,Y)\in[0,1]$, for $w\in\mathcal W$, be Borel functions of a $k\times d$ matrix and a vector in $\mathbb R^k$ with $\sum_wK_w\le1$. Define $$\label{p:cap-reference-probabilities}
 \begin{split}
 f_w(s)&=\mathbb E_X K_w(X,Xs),\\
 q_w&=\mathbb E_X\int K_w(X,Xc+rHz)\,dm_k(z).
 \end{split}$$ The functions $f_w$ are measurable. The variables $z$ in the second line are independent of $X$ and are used only to define a reference law. The kernel still receives the full matrix $X$ and the radial factor $H$ through the displayed label. In particular, $\sum_wq_w\le1$.

<a id="stmt-p-cap-domination"></a>
<div id="p:cap-domination" class="proposition">

**Proposition 16** (Positive domination after one block). *For each $w\in\mathcal W$, there are countably many caps $B(c_i,\rho_i)$ with $0<\rho_i\le r$ and coefficients $a_i\ge0$ such that <a id="p:cap-domination-bound"></a>$$\label{p:cap-domination-bound}
 f_w\mu_{c,r}\le\sum_i a_i\mu_{c_i,\rho_i},
 \qquad
 \sum_i a_i(r/\rho_i)^\alpha\le\exp(Cd)q_w^\theta.$$ The first inequality is an inequality of finite positive measures on $S^{d-1}$: it holds after evaluating both sides on every measurable set. The constant $C$ is independent of the parent cap and of the kernels.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-p-cap-domination"><summary>Proof of stmt-p-cap-domination</summary>

*Proof.* We prove a bound for nonnegative tests and then convert it into a countable positive domination.

<a id="p:cap-dual-constraints"></a>
*Cap tests and their local mass.* Put $\rho_j=r2^{-j}$ and choose a finite $\rho_j/2$-net $N_j$ of the sphere for every $j\ge0$. Take as a dictionary the parent cap, indexed by $*$, together with all $B(c',\rho_j)$, $c'\in N_j$. Repetitions may have separate indices. For this countable index set $I$, write $B_i=B(c_i,\rho_i)$ and $$\phi_i=\frac{\mathbf 1_{B(c_i,\rho_i)}}{\sigma(B(c_i,\rho_i))},
 \qquad b_i=(r/\rho_i)^\alpha.$$ Thus $\phi_*\,d\sigma=\mu_{c,r}$, every $\phi_i$ is a probability density, $b_*=1$, and the weights at level $j$ are $2^{\alpha j}$. If $h\in L^\infty(\sigma)$ is nonnegative and satisfies $$\label{p:cap-dual-constraints}
 \int h\phi_i\,d\sigma\le b_i\qquad(i\in I),$$ then its integral against any positive dictionary sum is at most that sum’s weighted cost. We will bound $\int h f_w\,d\mu_{c,r}$ for every such test.

<a id="p:cap-test-ball-bound"></a>
Let $\nu$ be the image of $h\mu_{c,r}$ under $s\mapsto(s-c)/r$. It has unit-ball support and mass at most one by the parent constraint. We claim that, for an absolute $D\ge1$, $$\label{p:cap-test-ball-bound}
 \nu(D(b,t))\le D^d t^\lambda
 \qquad(b\in\mathbb R^d,\ t>0).$$ For $0<t\le1/4$, suppose the ball meets the rescaled cap, and choose a point $s_0\in B$ whose image lies in it. The corresponding sphere points are all within $2rt$ of $s_0$. Set $$j=\left\lfloor\log_2\frac1{4t}\right\rfloor,\qquad \rho=\rho_j;
 \qquad 4rt\le\rho<8rt,\quad \rho\le r.$$ A center in $N_j$ within $\rho/2$ of $s_0$ gives a dictionary cap containing those points, since $2rt+\rho/2\le\rho$. Its constraint and the two-sided cap estimate imply $$\begin{aligned}
 \nu(D(b,t))
 &\le\frac{\sigma(B_i)}{\sigma(B)}\int h\,d\mu_{c_i,\rho_i}\\
 &\le(A_0/a)^n(\rho/r)^{n-\alpha}
 \le(A_0/a)^n(8t)^\lambda.
\end{aligned}$$ An empty intersection has zero mass. For $t>1/4$, use the mass bound and $1\le4^\lambda t^\lambda$. These estimates prove <a href="#p:cap-test-ball-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:cap-test-ball-bound">[p:cap-test-ball-bound]</a>.

<a id="p:cap-dual-test"></a>
*The quantitative test estimate.* All hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#p:frame-positive" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:frame-positive">12</a> now hold for $\nu$. Indeed, the test, parent cap, and measure are fixed before the fresh matrix $X$ is drawn. For every orthonormal $k$-frame $P$, the coordinate density in Lemma <a href="#p:coordinate-density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:coordinate-density">1</a> gives $P_\#\sigma\ll dz$. Because $h\mu_{c,r}\ll\sigma$, its projection is also absolutely continuous, and the affine rescaling $$P((s-c)/r)=(Ps-Pc)/r$$ gives the same conclusion for $P_\#\nu$. The theorem therefore supplies a jointly measurable density satisfying, for almost every $X$, $$P_\#\nu=g_Xm_k,\qquad g_X(z)=0\text{ for }\left\lVert z\right\rVert>1,
 \qquad \mathbb E_X\int g_X^p\,dm_k\le\exp(C_Ddp).$$ The density is relative to $dm_k=dz/v_k$ on the unit ball; $D$, and hence the new constant, is absolute. Since $s=c+ru$ gives $Xs=Xc+rHPu$, Tonelli’s theorem and Hölder’s inequality on the product law of $X$ and $z$ yield $$\label{p:cap-dual-test}
\begin{split}
 \int h f_w\,d\mu_{c,r}
 &=\mathbb E_X\int K_w(X,Xc+rHz)g_X(z)\,dm_k(z)\\
 &\le\left(\mathbb E_X\int g_X^p\,dm_k\right)^{1/p}
 \left(\mathbb E_X\int K_w(X,Xc+rHz)^{p'}\,dm_k\right)^{1/p'}\\
 &\le\exp(Cd)q_w^\theta,\qquad p'=p/(p-1).
\end{split}$$ The last line uses $0\le K_w\le1$. Thus every test satisfying <a href="#p:cap-dual-constraints" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:cap-dual-constraints">[p:cap-dual-constraints]</a> obeys <a href="#p:cap-dual-test" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:cap-dual-test">[p:cap-dual-test]</a>.

<a id="p:cap-compact-tail"></a>
*Representation by positive cap sums.* The geometric estimate is complete; the remaining step uses only the dictionary and its test bound. If $q_w=0$, the feasible test $h=1$ shows that $f_w\mu_{c,r}=0$, and the empty sum suffices. Otherwise set $K=\exp(Cd)q_w^\theta>0$, with the constant from <a href="#p:cap-dual-test" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:cap-dual-test">[p:cap-dual-test]</a>, and define in real $L^1(\sigma)$ $$\mathcal C_K=\left\{\sum_{i\in I}a_i\phi_i:
 a_i\ge0,\ \sum_i a_ib_i\le K\right\}.$$ Each series converges in $L^1$, since $b_i\ge1$ and $\left\lVert \phi_i\right\rVert_{L^1}=1$. This convex set is norm compact. To see this, take a sequence and choose a coefficient array for each term. A diagonal subsequence converges coefficientwise, since $0\le a_i\le K/b_i$. The limiting array has cost at most $K$ by Fatou’s lemma. There are finitely many indices through every level $J$, and uniformly over all admissible arrays, $$\label{p:cap-compact-tail}
 \left\|\sum_{j>J}\sum_{i\text{ at level }j}a_i\phi_i\right\|_{L^1}
 =\sum_{j>J}\sum_{i\text{ at level }j}a_i
 \le K2^{-\alpha(J+1)}.$$ The same estimate holds for the limiting array. Finite-level convergence followed by <a href="#p:cap-compact-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:cap-compact-tail">[p:cap-compact-tail]</a> gives convergence in $L^1$. This proves sequential compactness, and hence compactness in the metric space $L^1(\sigma)$ (Schnaubelt 2025, sec. 1.3, Theorem 1.37, p. 29) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[SchnaubeltFA2025](#ref-SchnaubeltFA2025)].

Its signed downward closure $$\mathcal D_K=\{u\in L^1(\sigma):u\le v\text{ a.e. for some }v\in\mathcal C_K\}$$ is convex, contains every nonpositive $L^1$ function, and is norm closed. In fact, if $u_m\to u$ and $u_m\le v_m\in\mathcal C_K$, compactness provides a subsequence $v_m\to v\in\mathcal C_K$; then $$\left\lVert (u-v)_+\right\rVert_{L^1}
 \le\left\lVert u-u_m\right\rVert_{L^1}+\left\lVert v_m-v\right\rVert_{L^1}\longrightarrow0.$$ Put $F=f_w\phi_*$. If $F\notin\mathcal D_K$, strict separation of the closed convex set $\mathcal D_K$ and the compact singleton $\{F\}$ (Schnaubelt 2025, sec. 5.2, Theorem 5.20(b), pp. 98–99) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[SchnaubeltFA2025](#ref-SchnaubeltFA2025)] gives a continuous real functional $\ell$ with $$\ell(F)>\sup_{u\in\mathcal D_K}\ell(u).$$ A probability measure is localizable (Fremlin, n.d.a, Theorem 211L), so real $L^1$–$L^\infty$ duality (Fremlin, n.d.b, Theorem 243G(b)) represents $\ell(u)=\int hu\,d\sigma$ for a real $h\in L^\infty(\sigma)$. The finite supremum forces $h\ge0$: if $h\le-\delta$ on a set $E$ of positive measure, the functions $-t\mathbf 1_E\in\mathcal D_K$ would make it infinite. Define $$S_h=\sup_i\frac{\int h\phi_i\,d\sigma}{b_i},\qquad
 0\le S_h\le\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{L^\infty}.$$ Then <a id="p:cap-support-functional"></a>$$\label{p:cap-support-functional}
 \sup_{u\in\mathcal D_K}\int hu\,d\sigma=KS_h.$$ The cost bound gives the upper inequality, and the single terms $(K/b_i)\phi_i$ give the reverse inequality by taking the supremum over $i$. This does not require the supremum to be attained. If $S_h=0$, the parent term gives $\int h\phi_*\,d\sigma=0$, and $0\le f_w\le1$ implies $\ell(F)=0$, contrary to strict separation. If $S_h>0$, the function $h/S_h$ satisfies <a href="#p:cap-dual-constraints" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:cap-dual-constraints">[p:cap-dual-constraints]</a>, so <a href="#p:cap-dual-test" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:cap-dual-test">[p:cap-dual-test]</a> again contradicts separation. Therefore $F\in\mathcal D_K$. An admissible series dominates its density almost everywhere; integrating gives the asserted measure inequality and cost bound. Its total mass is at most $K$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### Propagation, bounded stopping, and the accuracy endpoint

The preceding proposition applies to each cap in a joint-state bound. The positivity of all measures makes the iteration linear before any estimate is taken.

<a id="stmt-p-cap-forward-step"></a>
<div id="p:cap-forward-step" class="lemma">

<a id="p:cap-cost-multiplier"></a>
**Lemma 17** (Cost of a forward block). *Let $\mathcal V$ and $\mathcal W$ be finite state sets with $\left\lvert \mathcal W\right\rvert\le2^M$. For each $v\in\mathcal V$, let $K_{v,w}$ be block kernels of the type in <a href="#p:cap-reference-probabilities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:cap-reference-probabilities">[p:cap-reference-probabilities]</a>, with likelihoods $f_{v,w}$. Let $\eta_v$ be finite positive measures on the sphere and suppose $$\eta_v\le\sum_i a_{v,i}\mu_{c_{v,i},r_{v,i}},\qquad
 \mathcal P=\sum_{v,i}a_{v,i}(2/r_{v,i})^\alpha<\infty,$$ where each sum is countable, $a_{v,i}\ge0$, and $0<r_{v,i}\le2$. Define the outgoing measures by $$\eta'_w(E)=\sum_v\int_E f_{v,w}(s)\,\eta_v(ds).$$ Then the $\eta'_w$ admit countable positive cap dominations whose total cost $\mathcal P'$ satisfies $$\label{p:cap-cost-multiplier}
 \mathcal P'\le\mathcal P\exp(Cd)2^{M/p}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-p-cap-forward-step"><summary>Proof of stmt-p-cap-forward-step</summary>

*Proof.* Multiplication by a nonnegative measurable function preserves a measure inequality, as follows first for simple functions and then by monotone convergence. Summing over incoming states and cap terms therefore gives $$\eta'_w\le\sum_{v,i}a_{v,i}f_{v,w}\mu_{c_{v,i},r_{v,i}}.$$ Apply Proposition <a href="#p:cap-domination" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:cap-domination">16</a> separately to every term. For a fixed incoming cap, write $q_{v,i,w}$ for its reference probabilities. They depend on that cap as well as on the starting state, and satisfy $\sum_wq_{v,i,w}\le1$. Hölder’s inequality for the finite sum gives $$\sum_wq_{v,i,w}^{\theta}
 \le\left\lvert \mathcal W\right\rvert^{1/p}\left(\sum_wq_{v,i,w}\right)^\theta
 \le2^{M/p}.$$ If the outgoing terms for this parent have coefficients $\beta_j$ and radii $\rho_j$, their contribution to the global cost is bounded by $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_j a_{v,i}\beta_j(2/\rho_j)^\alpha
 &=a_{v,i}(2/r_{v,i})^\alpha
       \sum_j \beta_j(r_{v,i}/\rho_j)^\alpha\\
 &\le a_{v,i}(2/r_{v,i})^\alpha\exp(Cd)q_{v,i,w}^\theta.
\end{aligned}$$ Sum first over $w$ and then over the countably many incoming terms to obtain <a href="#p:cap-cost-multiplier" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:cap-cost-multiplier">[p:cap-cost-multiplier]</a>. Tonelli’s theorem applies throughout because every summand is nonnegative.

At each of finitely many block boundaries there are only countably many state–cap terms. Choosing a dominating sum for each produces a deterministic proof representation, with another countable sum at the next boundary. There is no choice indexed by the random signal or by the fresh matrix. The state measures here remain unnormalized joint measures; the argument never divides them by their total masses. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-p-cap-terminal"></a>
<div id="p:cap-terminal" class="lemma">

**Lemma 18** (Terminal success under a cap). *Let $Q$ be any probability law on unit-vector outputs, independent of the signal, and put $h_Q(s)=Q\{z:\arccos\langle z,s\rangle\le\epsilon\}$ for $0<\epsilon\le1/10$. For every cap $B(c,r)$, <a id="p:cap-terminal-bound"></a>$$\label{p:cap-terminal-bound}
 \int h_Q\,d\mu_{c,r}\le\exp(Cd)(\epsilon/r)^\alpha.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-p-cap-terminal"><summary>Proof of stmt-p-cap-terminal</summary>

*Proof.* For a fixed unit output $z$, write $\vartheta=\arccos\langle z,s\rangle$. Angular success implies $\left\lVert s-z\right\rVert=2\sin(\vartheta/2)\le\epsilon$. If $\epsilon<r$, Lemma <a href="#p:cap-mass" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:cap-mass">3</a> bounds its probability under $\mu_{c,r}$ by $$\frac{\sigma(B(z,\epsilon))}{\sigma(B(c,r))}
 \le(A_0/a)^n(\epsilon/r)^n
 \le\exp(Cd)(\epsilon/r)^\alpha.$$ If $\epsilon\ge r$, use probability at most one and $(\epsilon/r)^\alpha\ge1$. Integration over $Q$ proves the result. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-p-cap-fixed-stop"></a>
<div id="p:cap-fixed-stop" class="proposition">

<a id="p:cap-fixed-stop-explicit"></a>
<a id="p:cap-fixed-stop-asymptotic"></a>
**Proposition 19** (A fixed stopping index). *For all sufficiently large $d$, let a learner obey the observation and information restrictions in Section <a href="#p:geometry-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:geometry-model">2</a>, with $0<\epsilon\le1/10$, any nonnegative integer $M$ bits of state, and horizon $T$, and let $\mathsf T$ be its stopping index. For every fixed integer $0\le t\le T$, $$\label{p:cap-fixed-stop-explicit}
 \mathbb P\{\mathsf T=t\text{ and }\arccos\langle \widehat S,S\rangle\le\epsilon\}
 \le\exp(Cd)(\epsilon/2)^\alpha
       \left[\exp(Cd)2^{M/p}\right]^{\lceil t/k\rceil}.$$ In particular, for a sequence $M(d)=o(d^2)$ the right side is eventually at most $$\label{p:cap-fixed-stop-asymptotic}
 \exp\bigl(Cd(1+\lceil t/k\rceil)\bigr)(\epsilon/2)^\alpha,$$ with an absolute $C$ and a dimension threshold that may depend on the memory sequence.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-p-cap-fixed-stop"><summary>Proof of stmt-p-cap-fixed-stop</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $t>0$ throughout the argument and split its first $t$ indices into $b=\lceil t/k\rceil$ consecutive blocks of length at most $k$. At a prefinal boundary retain only paths that have not stopped and are eligible to continue. In the final block retain exactly the paths stopping at $t$, with their terminal state. For a fixed starting state and a supplied block of pairs, integrating the internal randomness gives the probability of each retained ending state. These are Borel kernels with sum at most one, since all omitted paths are discarded. A shorter block is padded by independent unused Gaussian rows, which the kernels ignore. Its likelihood for a signal $s$ is therefore $f_{v,w}(s)$ of <a href="#p:cap-reference-probabilities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:cap-reference-probabilities">[p:cap-reference-probabilities]</a>. Conditional on the fixed seed, the fresh Gaussian feature rows in the block are jointly independent of the signal and entering state, indeed of the signal, initialization, past rows, and past coins. Given the signal, the past affects this likelihood only through the starting state and the fixed index.

At any counted boundary, let $\eta_v(E)$ be the probability that $S\in E$ and the path is counted in state $v$. These are unnormalized joint measures and their outgoing laws have exactly the formula in Lemma <a href="#p:cap-forward-step" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:cap-forward-step">17</a>. Initially, $B(c,2)=S^{d-1}$ and $\mu_{c,2}=\sigma$. Write $V_0$ for the initial finite state. For the fixed shared seed $\xi$, joint initialization independence gives $$\eta_v^\xi(E)=\pi_\xi(v)\sigma(E),
 \qquad \pi_\xi(v)=\mathbb P\{V_0=v\mid\xi\},\qquad
 \sum_v\pi_\xi(v)=1.$$ Thus the initial total cost is one before any discarded paths are removed, and at most one afterward. After $b$ applications of that lemma, the counted terminal measures admit positive cap dominations of total cost at most $[\exp(Cd)2^{M/p}]^b$.

At each fixed terminal state and index $t$, the output is drawn from a fixed law using fresh randomness. Apply Lemma <a href="#p:cap-terminal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:cap-terminal">18</a> to each term of its dominating cap sum. A term of coefficient $a$ and radius $r$ contributes at most $\exp(Cd)(\epsilon/2)^\alpha a(2/r)^\alpha$. Summing proves the conditional version of <a href="#p:cap-fixed-stop-explicit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:cap-fixed-stop-explicit">[p:cap-fixed-stop-explicit]</a>. At $t=0$, use the initial domination and the same terminal estimate directly. The index has been fixed throughout, so only the at most $2^M$ states at each ending boundary were counted. No additional state is charged for the value of $t$.

The countable dominating sums are chosen only after fixing the shared seed. The bound is uniform in that seed, so the common convention of Section <a href="#p:geometry-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:geometry-model">2</a> allows us to average the original conditional success probabilities. No measurable choice of cap decompositions across shared seeds is required. Finally, for $M(d)=o(d^2)$ we eventually have $M(d)\le d^2$. Since $p\ge d/32$, the logarithm of the extra factor $2^{M/p}$ is then at most $32(\log2)d$. Absorbing it into the absolute exponential constant proves <a href="#p:cap-fixed-stop-asymptotic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:cap-fixed-stop-asymptotic">[p:cap-fixed-stop-asymptotic]</a>. ◻

</details>

We now sum the fixed-index estimate over all possible stopping times and use the common full-data bound to absorb the resulting lower-order terms.

<details id="proof-stmt-p-cap-fixed-stop-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-p-cap-fixed-stop</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#p:regression" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:regression">4</a> by positive cap domination.* Put $L=\log(1/\epsilon)$.

For the given sequence $M(d)=o(d^2)$, use <a href="#p:cap-fixed-stop-asymptotic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:cap-fixed-stop-asymptotic">[p:cap-fixed-stop-asymptotic]</a> in all sufficiently large dimensions. The stopping events are disjoint. Since $k\ge d/32$, $\alpha\ge d/3$, and $\lceil t/k\rceil\le t/k+1$, summing over $0\le t\le T$ yields <a id="p:cap-total-success"></a>$$\label{p:cap-total-success}
 \mathbb P\{\text{success}\}
 \le(T+1)\exp(C_1d+C_2T-dL/3).$$ Here $(\epsilon/2)^\alpha\le\exp(-dL/3)$, and $C_1,C_2$ are absolute. If success is at least $2/3$, taking logarithms gives $$\frac{dL}{3}
 \le C_1d+C_2T+\log(T+1)+\log(3/2)
 \le C_1d+(C_2+1)T+\log(3/2),$$ since $\log(T+1)\le T$ for every $T\ge0$. Lemma <a href="#p:haar-linear" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:haar-linear">7</a> also gives $T>d/8$, and therefore $T\ge1$. Consequently $$dL\le3(8C_1+C_2+2)T.$$ This proves the claimed bound with an absolute constant, uniformly over the full accuracy range. Only the dimension at which the memory factor is absorbed may depend on the sequence $M(d)$. ◻

</details>

<a id="p:riesz-section"></a>
### Riesz estimates at fixed affine offsets

An integrated density norm does not specify the value of a density on a matrix-dependent graph of labels. We now construct a single density version whose moment is controlled at every fixed affine offset. The bound uses the supremum of ambient Riesz potentials of the original measure. We first prove this analytic estimate, then use it on distance shells to propagate finite-state continuation probabilities backward. The shell argument retains an explicit logarithmic loss.

Throughout this section, fix an integer $d\ge16$ and put <a id="p:riesz-parameters"></a>$$\label{p:riesz-parameters}
 k=q=\lfloor d/8\rfloor,\qquad \alpha=d/2.$$ These choices give $$q\ge2,\qquad d/16\le k=q\le d/8,\qquad
 \alpha-k-q\ge1,\qquad d-1-\alpha\ge1.$$ The matrix $X$ will always denote a $k$ by $d$ standard Gaussian matrix. Constants denoted by $C\ge1$ are absolute and may increase between uses.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Ambient potentials and affine distances

<a id="p:riesz-potential"></a>
For a measurable $h:S^{d-1}\to[0,1]$, define the supremum of its ambient Riesz potentials by $$\label{p:riesz-potential}
 V(h)=\sup_{z\in\mathbb R^d}
       \int_{S^{d-1}}\frac{h(s)}{\left\lVert s-z\right\rVert^{\alpha}}\,d\sigma(s).$$ For a measure $\mu$, the raw Riesz potential is $U_\alpha^\mu(z)=\int\left\lVert s-z\right\rVert^{-\alpha}\,d\mu(s)$ (Calef and Hardin 2008, sec. 1, p. 2) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[CalefHardin2008v1](#ref-CalefHardin2008v1)]. Thus $V(h)$ is its supremum over ambient centers for $\mu=h\sigma$; it is not the Riesz energy. The kernel is understood as a nonnegative extended-valued function at $s=z$. Write $B_r(z)=\{x\in\mathbb R^d:\left\lVert x-z\right\rVert\le r\}$, and intersect such balls with the sphere when integrating against $\sigma$.

<a id="stmt-p-riesz-potential-finite"></a>
<div id="p:riesz-potential-finite" class="lemma">

<a id="p:riesz-cap"></a>
<a id="p:riesz-inner"></a>
**Lemma 20** (Ambient potential bounds). *For every $z\in\mathbb R^d$ and $0<r\le1/10$, $$\label{p:riesz-cap}
 \sigma(B_r(z))\le C^d r^{d-1}.$$ Every measurable $h:S^{d-1}\to[0,1]$ has finite potential. Moreover, for $0<\epsilon\le1/10$, $$\label{p:riesz-inner}
 \sup_{z\in\mathbb R^d}
 \int_{\left\lVert s-z\right\rVert\le\epsilon}\left\lVert s-z\right\rVert^{-\alpha}\,d\sigma(s)
 \le C^d\epsilon^{d-1-\alpha}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-p-riesz-potential-finite"><summary>Proof of stmt-p-riesz-potential-finite</summary>

*Proof.* The ambient estimate <a href="#p:riesz-cap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-cap">[p:riesz-cap]</a> follows from Lemma <a href="#p:cap-mass" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:cap-mass">3</a>, with $2^{d-1}$ absorbed into $C^d$. For dyadic outer radii $r_m=\epsilon2^{-m}$, the shell $r_m/2<\left\lVert s-z\right\rVert\le r_m$ contributes at most $(2/r_m)^\alpha C^d r_m^{d-1}$. The point $s=z$ has zero $\sigma$-measure, and $\sum_{m\ge0}2^{-m(d-1-\alpha)}\le2$. Summing proves <a href="#p:riesz-inner" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-inner">[p:riesz-inner]</a>. The same argument at radius $1/10$, together with the kernel bound $10^\alpha$ outside that ball, proves $V(h)<\infty$ uniformly over $0\le h\le1$. ◻

</details>

The potential bounds the measure of a tube around any affine subspace through an ambient center. We use that fact to integrate successive inverse distances when computing the Gaussian projection moment.

<a id="stmt-p-riesz-distance"></a>
<div id="p:riesz-distance" class="lemma">

<a id="p:riesz-distance-bound"></a>
**Lemma 21** (Inverse distance to an anchored affine space). *Let $h:S^{d-1}\to[0,1]$ be measurable, $z\in\mathbb R^d$, $\rho>0$, and let $F$ be an affine subspace through $z$ of dimension $\ell\le q-1$. Then $F\cap B_\rho(z)$ has zero $h\,d\sigma$ mass, and $$\label{p:riesz-distance-bound}
 \int_{B_\rho(z)}
       \operatorname{dist}(s,F)^{-k}h(s)\,d\sigma(s)
 \le C^d V(h)\rho^{\alpha-k}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-p-riesz-distance"><summary>Proof of stmt-p-riesz-distance</summary>

<a id="p:riesz-ball-control"></a>
<a id="p:riesz-tube"></a>
*Proof.* For every ambient center $c$ and radius $r>0$, definition <a href="#p:riesz-potential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-potential">[p:riesz-potential]</a> gives $$\label{p:riesz-ball-control}
 \int_{B_r(c)}h\,d\sigma\le V(h)r^\alpha .$$ Fix $0<r\le\rho$. A maximal $r$-separated subset of $F\cap B_\rho(z)$ has at most $(1+2\rho/r)^\ell
\le(3\rho/r)^\ell$ points, by comparing disjoint radius-$r/2$ balls in $F$ with the radius-$(\rho+r/2)$ ball. It is an $r$-net. The orthogonal projection onto $F$ of any point in $B_\rho(z)$ lies in $F\cap B_\rho(z)$, since $z\in F$. Consequently the part of $B_\rho(z)$ within distance $r$ of $F$ is covered by the corresponding ambient radius-$2r$ balls. Equation <a href="#p:riesz-ball-control" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-ball-control">[p:riesz-ball-control]</a> yields $$\label{p:riesz-tube}
 \int_{B_\rho(z)\cap\{\operatorname{dist}(s,F)\le r\}}
       h(s)\,d\sigma(s)
 \le 3^\ell2^\alpha V(h)\rho^\ell r^{\alpha-\ell}.$$ This includes $\ell=0$, with a one-point net. Since $\alpha-\ell>0$, letting $r$ decrease to zero shows that $F\cap B_\rho(z)$ has zero mass.

Every point of $B_\rho(z)$ is within distance $\rho$ of $F$. Apply <a href="#p:riesz-tube" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-tube">[p:riesz-tube]</a> on the shells with outer distance $r_m=\rho2^{-m}$ and bound the inverse power there by $(2/r_m)^k$. Their sum is at most $$3^\ell2^{\alpha+k}V(h)\rho^{\alpha-k}
 \sum_{m\ge0}2^{-m(\alpha-\ell-k)}.$$ Here $\alpha-\ell-k\ge\alpha-(q-1)-k\ge2$, so the series is at most $2$. Since $\ell\le d/8$, $\alpha=d/2$, and $k\le d/8$, the remaining constant is at most $C^d$. This proves <a href="#p:riesz-distance-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-distance-bound">[p:riesz-distance-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### A density at fixed affine offsets

The next estimate uses $q$ points anchored at the chosen center $z$. Their images under each Gaussian row have an anchored Gram matrix as covariance. The inverse-distance bound just proved will integrate the negative determinant power in the joint Gaussian density.

<a id="stmt-p-riesz-density"></a>
<div id="p:riesz-density" class="lemma">

<a id="p:riesz-density-bound"></a>
<a id="p:riesz-density-support"></a>
**Lemma 22** (Projection density at deterministic offsets). *Let $z\in\mathbb R^d$, $\rho>0$, let $H\subset S^{d-1}\cap B_\rho(z)$ be measurable, and let $h:S^{d-1}\to[0,1]$ be measurable. Fix these objects before drawing a $k$ by $d$ standard Gaussian matrix $X$. The pushforward of $h\mathbf 1_H\,d\sigma$ by $s\mapsto Xs$ admits a single jointly Borel function $$R:\mathbb R^{k\times d}\times\mathbb R^k\longrightarrow[0,\infty]$$ such that $R(X,\cdot)$ is its Lebesgue density for every full-row-rank $X$, and, for every fixed $b\in\mathbb R^k$, $$\label{p:riesz-density-bound}
 \left(\mathbb E_X R(X,Xz+b)^q\right)^{1/q}
 \le C^d V(h)\rho^{\alpha-k}.$$ The same function $R$ is used for all $b$. Moreover, $$\label{p:riesz-density-support}
 R(X,y)=0
 \quad\hbox{if}\quad
 \left\lVert y-Xz\right\rVert>\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\rho .$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-p-riesz-density"><summary>Proof of stmt-p-riesz-density</summary>

<a id="p:riesz-ball-average"></a>
*Proof.* Let $\mu$ be the finite Borel measure given by $\mu(E)=\int_E h\mathbf1_H\,d\sigma$ on Borel subsets of the sphere. For $\eta>0$, let $B_\eta^k=\{u\in\mathbb R^k:\left\lVert u\right\rVert\le\eta\}$ and let $\operatorname{vol}_k$ denote $k$-dimensional Lebesgue measure. Define $$\label{p:riesz-ball-average}
 R_\eta(X,y)=
 \frac{1}{\operatorname{vol}_k(B_\eta^k)}
 \int \mathbf1_{\{\left\lVert Xt-y\right\rVert\le\eta\}}\,d\mu(t).$$ The integrand is Borel in $(X,y,t)$, so the parameter integral is Borel in $(X,y)$. We first prove <a href="#p:riesz-density-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-density-bound">[p:riesz-density-bound]</a> with $R_\eta$ in place of $R$, uniformly in $\eta$.

Expand its $q$th moment at $Xz+b$ by Tonelli’s theorem, using $t_1,\ldots,t_q$ from the $q$ copies of $\mu$. For a fixed tuple, the columns $X(t_j-z)$ form an array whose $k$ rows are independent centered Gaussian vectors with covariance $$G=\bigl(\langle t_i-z,t_j-z\rangle\bigr)_{1\le i,j\le q}.$$ If $G$ is nonsingular, their joint density is bounded everywhere by $(2\pi)^{-kq/2}(\det G)^{-k/2}$. Therefore the probability that all columns belong to the radius-$\eta$ ball about $b$ is at most this bound times $\operatorname{vol}_k(B_\eta^k)^q$.

<a id="p:riesz-gram"></a>
The singular tuples have zero $\mu^q$ mass. Indeed, at the first linearly dependent vector $t_j-z$, the point $t_j$ lies in $z+\operatorname{span}(t_1-z,\ldots,t_{j-1}-z)$, an affine space through $z$ of dimension at most $q-1$. Lemma <a href="#p:riesz-distance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:riesz-distance">21</a> assigns zero $\mu$ mass to it. The degeneracy set is measurable, and Fubini’s theorem applied at each $j$ proves the claim. For a nonsingular tuple, Gram–Schmidt triangularizes the matrix with columns $t_j-z$ with diagonal entries equal to the successive orthogonal distances. Taking the determinant of its Gram matrix gives $$\label{p:riesz-gram}
 (\det G)^{-k/2}
 =\prod_{j=1}^q
 \operatorname{dist}\!\left(
 t_j,z+\operatorname{span}(t_1-z,\ldots,t_{j-1}-z)
 \right)^{-k}.$$ Integrate this product first in $t_q$, then in $t_{q-1}$, and so on. For every nonsingular prefix, Lemma <a href="#p:riesz-distance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:riesz-distance">21</a> bounds the next integral by $C^dV(h)\rho^{\alpha-k}$. The exceptional prefixes have zero product measure by the same degeneracy argument. It follows that the integral of <a href="#p:riesz-gram" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-gram">[p:riesz-gram]</a> is at most $(C^dV(h)\rho^{\alpha-k})^q$. The ball volumes cancel in the moment expansion, and the Gaussian prefactor is at most one. Thus $$\mathbb E_X R_\eta(X,Xz+b)^q
 \le \bigl(C^dV(h)\rho^{\alpha-k}\bigr)^q$$ for every $\eta>0$ and every fixed $b$.

<a id="p:riesz-density-version"></a>
Now choose the function once and for all: $$\label{p:riesz-density-version}
 R(X,y)=\liminf_{m\to\infty}R_{1/m}(X,y).$$ It is jointly Borel. To see that it is a density, fix a full-row-rank $X$. Set $D=(XX^{\mathsf T})^{1/2}$ and $P=D^{-1}X$, so $D$ is invertible, $P$ has orthonormal rows, and $X=DP$. Lemma <a href="#p:coordinate-density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:coordinate-density">1</a> makes $P_\#\sigma$ absolutely continuous, and the invertible map $D$ preserves absolute continuity. Since $\mu\le\sigma$, the pushforward $X_\#\mu$ is also absolutely continuous. The Lebesgue differentiation theorem (Tao 2011, Theorem 1.6.19) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[TaoMeasure2011](#ref-TaoMeasure2011)], applied to its $L^1$ density, shows that <a href="#p:riesz-density-version" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-density-version">[p:riesz-density-version]</a> equals that density almost everywhere in $y$. A Gaussian $X$ has full row rank almost surely, since each successive Gaussian row avoids the span of the preceding rows.

For each fixed $b$, the identity $R(X,Xz+b)=\liminf_m R_{1/m}(X,Xz+b)$ holds by the definition of the single function $R$. Fatou’s lemma (Tao 2011, Corollary 1.4.47) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[TaoMeasure2011](#ref-TaoMeasure2011)] over $X$ now gives <a href="#p:riesz-density-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-density-bound">[p:riesz-density-bound]</a>. These are separate moment statements for each deterministic $b$; no simultaneous almost-sure bound in $X$ over all offsets is asserted. Finally, if $\left\lVert y-Xz\right\rVert>\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\rho$, then every $Xt$ with $t\in H$ is at distance at least $\left\lVert y-Xz\right\rVert-\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\rho>0$ from $y$. The averages in <a href="#p:riesz-ball-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-ball-average">[p:riesz-ball-average]</a> are eventually zero, proving <a href="#p:riesz-density-support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-density-support">[p:riesz-density-support]</a>. ◻

</details>

The explicit choice <a href="#p:riesz-density-version" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-density-version">[p:riesz-density-version]</a> is the feature needed for the next step. A density chosen only up to a Lebesgue-null set in $y$ for each $X$ would not justify the moment estimate at the matrix-dependent point $Xz+b$. An integrated $L^q(dX\,dy)$ estimate controls a density only up to product-null sets, while the graph $y=Xz+b$ has zero product measure. Positive cap domination instead controls the measure of every Borel set by a positive sum of cap measures. Neither conclusion supplies the specified-offset moment in Lemma <a href="#p:riesz-density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:riesz-density">22</a>; its anchored determinant estimate and explicit limit are therefore kept as a separate argument.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Continuation probabilities and the terminal potential

We apply the specified-offset estimate to the finite-state experiment of Section <a href="#p:geometry-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:geometry-model">2</a>. Fix nonnegative integers $M,T$ and an accuracy $0<\epsilon\le1/10$, and write $$p_{\mathrm{succ}}=\mathbb P\{\arccos\langle \widehat S,S\rangle\le\epsilon\},
 \qquad L=\log(1/\epsilon),\qquad
 N=\lceil T/k\rceil,\qquad W=(T+2)2^M.$$ The success probability averages over the uniform signal, Gaussian features, and learner randomness. The factor $W$ will count the retained states together with their possible terminal stopping indices.

<a id="stmt-p-riesz-success"></a>
<div id="p:riesz-success" class="proposition">

<a id="p:riesz-success-bound"></a>
**Proposition 23** (Riesz success estimate). *There is an absolute constant $C_0\ge1$ such that every learner just specified satisfies $$\label{p:riesz-success-bound}
 p_{\mathrm{succ}}\le B J^N,\qquad
 B=C_0^d\epsilon^{\,d/2-1},\qquad
 J=1+C_0^d(1+L)W^{1/q}.$$ This includes $T=0$. No asymptotic assumption on $M$ is needed for this estimate.*

</div>

We first bound the potential of each terminal output law. To control its increase across a block, we apply Lemma <a href="#p:riesz-density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:riesz-density">22</a> at $Xz+b$ and then integrate over $b$. The factor $1+L$ in <a href="#p:riesz-success-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-success-bound">[p:riesz-success-bound]</a> counts the distance shells and remains in the final parameter argument.

Use the fixed-seed and Borel-kernel convention of Section <a href="#p:geometry-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:geometry-model">2</a>: first fix an admissible shared seed, under which the initial state is independent of the signal and rows, and then apply Lemma <a href="#p:borel-kernels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:borel-kernels">6</a> to choose Borel rules. These rules define continuations from every state. All bounds below are uniform in the fixed rules; after proving the conditional bound, we average the original conditional success probabilities over the seed.

Complete unused rules and terminal laws arbitrarily, and force a halt by $T$. After a halt, retain a tag consisting of the stopping index and terminal state and ignore further samples. At any time there are at most $2^M$ live states and at most $(T+1)2^M$ such tags, including a possible immediate halt. Hence the stated bound $W$ applies. Add independent unused Gaussian rows after $T$ to complete the last block of length $k$. Let $\mathcal V_i$ be the state set at the boundary after block $i$, $0\le i\le N$; thus $\lvert\mathcal V_i\rvert\le W$.

<a id="p:riesz-recursion"></a>
For every $v\in\mathcal V_i$ and $s\in S^{d-1}$, let $h_i(v,s)$ be the success probability of the remaining execution started at that boundary state. This definition also applies to states not reached by the original run. At $i=N$ it is the success probability of the fixed output law associated with the terminal state. These functions are Borel and take values in $[0,1]$: the terminal assertion follows by integrating the Borel success indicator against the output law, and the assertion at earlier boundaries follows inductively from the formula below. If $X$ is a fresh $k$ by $d$ standard Gaussian matrix, then for $1\le i\le N$ $$\label{p:riesz-recursion}
 h_{i-1}(v,s)
 =\mathbb E_X\sum_{w\in\mathcal V_i}K^{(i)}_{v,w}(X,Xs)h_i(w,s).$$ Here $K^{(i)}_{v,w}(X,y)$ is the probability of the block transition from $v$ to $w$ on the exact row pairs $(X,y)$. These kernels are Borel, nonnegative, and sum to one in $w$ for every $(X,y)$. They incorporate internal randomization and the ignored rows after a halt. The functions $h_i(w,\cdot)$ are fixed independently of $X$. The fresh Gaussian block is independent of the signal, initialization, past rows, and past coins. Starting the continuation at the specified state therefore gives exactly <a href="#p:riesz-recursion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-recursion">[p:riesz-recursion]</a>; the state and boundary index contain all retained sample-dependent information.

<a id="stmt-p-riesz-terminal-lemma"></a>
<div id="p:riesz-terminal-lemma" class="lemma">

<a id="p:riesz-terminal"></a>
**Lemma 24** (Terminal potential). *Let $Q$ be a signal-independent probability law on unit-vector outputs, and let $h(s)=Q\{u:\arccos\langle u,s\rangle\le\epsilon\}$. Then $$\label{p:riesz-terminal}
 V(h)\le C^d\epsilon^{d-1-\alpha}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-p-riesz-terminal-lemma"><summary>Proof of stmt-p-riesz-terminal-lemma</summary>

*Proof.* Lemma <a href="#p:cap-mass" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:cap-mass">3</a> gives $\int h\,d\sigma\le\epsilon^{d-1}$, also for a randomized output law. For any ambient center $z$, use $h\le1$ and <a href="#p:riesz-inner" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-inner">[p:riesz-inner]</a> inside $B_\epsilon(z)$. Outside that ball, bound the kernel by $\epsilon^{-\alpha}$ and use the total mass bound. The sum is at most $C^d\epsilon^{d-1-\alpha}$ uniformly in $z$, proving <a href="#p:riesz-terminal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-terminal">[p:riesz-terminal]</a>. ◻

</details>

Set $$V_i=\max_{v\in\mathcal V_i}V(h_i(v,\cdot)).$$ The maximum is finite by Lemma <a href="#p:riesz-potential-finite" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:riesz-potential-finite">20</a>. Lemma <a href="#p:riesz-terminal-lemma" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:riesz-terminal-lemma">24</a> gives $V_N\le C^d\epsilon^{d-1-\alpha}$. We next estimate the contribution of a single distance shell in <a href="#p:riesz-recursion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-recursion">[p:riesz-recursion]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### One block and the distance shells

<a id="stmt-p-riesz-shell"></a>
<div id="p:riesz-shell" class="lemma">

<a id="p:riesz-shell-bound"></a>
**Lemma 25** (One shell across a block). *For $1\le i\le N$, $v\in\mathcal V_{i-1}$, $z\in\mathbb R^d$, and $\rho>0$, let $H_\rho(z)=\{s\in S^{d-1}:\rho/2<\left\lVert s-z\right\rVert\le\rho\}$. Then $$\label{p:riesz-shell-bound}
 \int_{H_\rho(z)}
   \frac{h_{i-1}(v,s)}{\left\lVert s-z\right\rVert^{\alpha}}\,d\sigma(s)
 \le C^d W^{1/q}V_i.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-p-riesz-shell"><summary>Proof of stmt-p-riesz-shell</summary>

<a id="p:riesz-shell-pushforward"></a>
*Proof.* For each successor $w$, use the particular density $R_w(X,y)$ of Lemma <a href="#p:riesz-density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:riesz-density">22</a> for the measure $h_i(w,\cdot)\mathbf1_{H_\rho(z)}\,d\sigma$. Tonelli’s theorem, <a href="#p:riesz-recursion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-recursion">[p:riesz-recursion]</a>, and pushforward integration give $$\label{p:riesz-shell-pushforward}
\begin{split}
 \int_{H_\rho(z)}
    \frac{h_{i-1}(v,s)}{\left\lVert s-z\right\rVert^{\alpha}}\,d\sigma(s)
 &\le (2/\rho)^\alpha
       \mathbb E_X\int_{\mathbb R^k}
       \sum_wK^{(i)}_{v,w}(X,y)R_w(X,y)\,dy\\
 &\le (2/\rho)^\alpha
       \mathbb E_X\int_{\left\lVert y-Xz\right\rVert\le\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\rho}
       \max_wR_w(X,y)\,dy .
\end{split}$$ The pushforward identity used in the first inequality holds for every full-row-rank $X$, hence almost surely. The second inequality uses the probability-vector property of $K^{(i)}$ and <a href="#p:riesz-density-support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-density-support">[p:riesz-density-support]</a>.

<a id="p:riesz-bins"></a>
We need a deterministic integration region after averaging over $X$. Choose a sufficiently large absolute $D_0$ and partition the matrices into $$E_1=\{\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\le D_0\sqrt d\},\qquad
 E_j=\{D_0(j-1)\sqrt d<\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\le D_0j\sqrt d\}
 \quad(j\ge2).$$ These bins obey $$\label{p:riesz-bins}
 \mathbb P(E_j)\le \exp\bigl(-4(j-1)^2d\bigr)\qquad(j\ge2).$$ For completeness, take $1/4$-nets on the unit spheres of $\mathbb R^d$ and $\mathbb R^k$. A maximal separated set has at most $9^d$ and $9^k$ points, respectively, by the disjoint-ball volume argument. Approximating each unit vector by its net point incurs an error at most $\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}/2$ in a bilinear form. Hence $\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}$ is at most twice the largest absolute bilinear form on the two nets. Each such form is standard Gaussian, so its elementary tail bound and a union bound give <a id="p:riesz-operator-tail"></a>$$\label{p:riesz-operator-tail}
 \mathbb P\{\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}>t\}
 \le 2\cdot9^{d+k}\exp(-t^2/8).$$ Putting $t=D_0(j-1)\sqrt d$ proves <a href="#p:riesz-bins" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-bins">[p:riesz-bins]</a> after increasing $D_0$, since $k\le d/8$.

<a id="p:riesz-bin-holder"></a>
For every deterministic $b\in\mathbb R^k$, Hölder’s inequality and <a href="#p:riesz-density-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-density-bound">[p:riesz-density-bound]</a> give $$\label{p:riesz-bin-holder}
\begin{split}
 \mathbb E_X\!\left[\mathbf1_{E_j}\max_wR_w(X,Xz+b)\right]
 &\le \mathbb P(E_j)^{1-1/q}
       \left(\sum_w\mathbb E_X R_w(X,Xz+b)^q\right)^{1/q}\\
 &\le \mathbb P(E_j)^{1-1/q}W^{1/q}
       C^dV_i\rho^{\alpha-k}.
\end{split}$$ No conditional version of the density lemma is being used: Hölder’s inequality pays for the bin event. For a fixed matrix in $E_j$, translate $y=Xz+b$ in <a href="#p:riesz-shell-pushforward" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-shell-pushforward">[p:riesz-shell-pushforward]</a>; the remaining region is $\left\lVert b\right\rVert\le D_0j\sqrt d\,\rho$. Joint measurability permits Tonelli’s theorem to integrate <a href="#p:riesz-bin-holder" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-bin-holder">[p:riesz-bin-holder]</a> over this deterministic ball.

The volume of that ball is at most $C^d j^k\rho^k$. Indeed, the unit-ball bound <a href="#p:unit-ball-volume" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:unit-ball-volume">[p:unit-ball-volume]</a> gives $v_k\le(2\pi e/k)^{k/2}$. Since $d/k\le16$, $v_k(D_0j\sqrt d\,\rho)^k\le C^d j^k\rho^k$. Also, by $q\ge2$ and <a href="#p:riesz-bins" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-bins">[p:riesz-bins]</a>, $$\sum_{j\ge1}j^k\mathbb P(E_j)^{1-1/q}
 \le 1+\sum_{j\ge2}
       \exp\bigl(k\log j-2(j-1)^2d\bigr)
 \le 2 .$$ For the last bound use $k\log j\le(d/8)(j-1)^2$ for $j\ge2$ and sum the resulting geometric upper bound. Combining the volume and bin estimates with <a href="#p:riesz-shell-pushforward" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-shell-pushforward">[p:riesz-shell-pushforward]</a> proves <a href="#p:riesz-shell-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-shell-bound">[p:riesz-shell-bound]</a>. The powers of the shell radius cancel exactly: $$\rho^{-\alpha}\rho^{\alpha-k}\rho^k=1.$$ ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-p-riesz-shell-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-p-riesz-shell</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#p:riesz-success" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:riesz-success">23</a>.* Outside $B_\epsilon(z)$ use the shells $$2^m\epsilon<\left\lVert s-z\right\rVert\le2^{m+1}\epsilon,
 \qquad m=0,1,\ldots .$$ For every ambient $z$, at most $C(1+L)$ of these meet the sphere. If $\left\lVert z\right\rVert\le3$, all distances are at most $4$, giving at most $\lceil\log_2(4/\epsilon)\rceil$ nonempty shells. If $\left\lVert z\right\rVert>3$, the distances lie between $\left\lVert z\right\rVert-1$ and $\left\lVert z\right\rVert+1$, whose ratio is less than $2$; only a bounded number of dyadic shells can then meet that interval.

<a id="p:riesz-step"></a>
Inside $B_\epsilon(z)$ use $h_{i-1}\le1$ and <a href="#p:riesz-inner" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-inner">[p:riesz-inner]</a>. On each remaining shell use Lemma <a href="#p:riesz-shell" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:riesz-shell">25</a>. Taking the supremum over $z$ and the maximum over $v$ gives $$\label{p:riesz-step}
 V_{i-1}\le C^d\epsilon^{d-1-\alpha}
          +C^d(1+L)W^{1/q}V_i .$$ Choose $C_0$ large enough that the first term here and the terminal value $V_N$ are at most $B$ in <a href="#p:riesz-success-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-success-bound">[p:riesz-success-bound]</a>, and that the second coefficient is at most $J-1$. With $U_i=\max\{V_i,B\}$, equation <a href="#p:riesz-step" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-step">[p:riesz-step]</a> gives $U_{i-1}\le J U_i$, while $U_N=B$. Hence $V_0\le BJ^N$, also when $N=0$.

At $z=0$ the kernel in <a href="#p:riesz-potential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-potential">[p:riesz-potential]</a> equals one on the unit sphere, so $\int h_0(v,s)\,d\sigma(s)\le V_0$ for each initial state. For almost every fixed shared seed, averaging over its conditional initial-state law preserves the bound, because that law is independent of the signal and rows. Averaging the original conditional success probabilities over the seed then preserves it again. This proves <a href="#p:riesz-success-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-success-bound">[p:riesz-success-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### The logarithmic lower bound

We now prove Theorem <a href="#p:regression" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:regression">4</a> from the Riesz success estimate. The parameter argument retains the stopping-index factor in $W$ and the shell-count factor $1+L$. Terminal-tag counting controls both in the only case still requiring proof, namely $T<dL$.

<details id="proof-stmt-p-riesz-shell-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-p-riesz-shell</summary>

<a id="p:riesz-packing"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#p:regression" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:regression">4</a> by the Riesz method.* There is a useful bound that counts possible terminal tags. For fixed rules as above, write $p_{\mathrm{succ},\mathrm{rule}}$ for the conditional success probability. For a tag $a$, let $f_a(s)$ be the probability of reaching it given $s$, and let $g_a(s)$ be its output law’s success probability. This output law depends only on the tag, and $0\le f_a\le1$. Lemma <a href="#p:cap-mass" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:cap-mass">3</a> gives $\int g_a\,d\sigma\le C^d\epsilon^{d-1}$, including a randomized output law. Sum over at most $W$ tags to obtain $$\label{p:riesz-packing}
 p_{\mathrm{succ},\mathrm{rule}}=\sum_a\int f_a(s)g_a(s)\,d\sigma(s)
 \le W C^d\epsilon^{d-1}.$$ The right side is independent of the chosen rules, so averaging also gives $p_{\mathrm{succ}}\le W C^d\epsilon^{d-1}$.

Write $L=\log(1/\epsilon)$. If $T\ge dL$, the conclusion already holds. In the remaining case $T<dL$, assume $p_{\mathrm{succ}}\ge2/3$. Taking logarithms in the averaged form of <a href="#p:riesz-packing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-packing">[p:riesz-packing]</a> gives $$(d-1)L
 \le M\log2+\log(T+2)+d\log C+\log(3/2).$$ For the given memory sequence, eventually $M\le d^2$. Since $L\ge\log10$ and $T<dL$, we also have $$\log(T+2)\le\log(2d)+\log L\le\log(2d)+L$$ for large $d$. It follows that $(d-2)L\le d^2\log2+d\log C+\log(2d)+\log(3/2)$. Consequently, only in this remaining case, <a id="p:riesz-short-run"></a>$$\label{p:riesz-short-run}
 L\le Cd,\qquad \log W=M\log2+\log(T+2)\le Cd^2 .$$ Thus the multiplier from Proposition <a href="#p:riesz-success" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:riesz-success">23</a> obeys $$\log J
 \le \log2+d\log C_0+\log(1+L)+\frac{\log W}{q}
 \le Cd ,$$ using $q\ge d/16$. In particular the shell-count term has been bounded rather than removed.

<a id="p:riesz-final"></a>
Now <a href="#p:riesz-success-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-success-bound">[p:riesz-success-bound]</a> and $p_{\mathrm{succ}}\ge2/3$ imply $$\label{p:riesz-final}
 (d/2-1)L
 \le d\log C_0+N\log J+\log(3/2)
 \le Cd(1+N)+\log(3/2)
 \le C'(d+T),$$ because $N=\lceil T/k\rceil\le16T/d+1$. The common full-data bound, Lemma <a href="#p:haar-linear" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:haar-linear">7</a>, gives $T>d/8$; hence the additive $d$ on the right of <a href="#p:riesz-final" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:riesz-final">[p:riesz-final]</a> is at most $8T$. For $d\ge4$ the left coefficient is at least $d/4$, so $T\ge c\,dL$ for an absolute $c>0$. This combines with the case $T\ge dL$ already separated. ◻

</details>

{
  "edges": [
    {
      "basis": "inferred",
      "direction": "directed",
      "explanation": "The ambient spherical cap mass upper bound provides the geometric measure growth condition on ambient balls needed to establish the mixed projection moment bounds for spherical measures.",
      "relation": "prerequisite",
      "source_anchor": "stmt-p-cap-mass",
      "source_pm_id": "PM-140.4",
      "source_quote": "For every ambient center $z\\in\\mathbb R^d$ and radius $r>0$,",
      "target_anchor": "thm:r-mixed-moment",
      "target_pm_id": "PM-140.5",
      "target_quote": "\\rho(B(z,t))\\le Dt^n.",
      "verification": "PENDING"
    },
    {
      "basis": "inferred",
      "direction": "directed",
      "explanation": "The finite memory tradeoff establishes an explicit sample lower bound for arbitrary memory budgets, which generalises the earlier bound that was restricted to memory at most a fixed quadratic multiple of the dimension.",
      "relation": "generalises",
      "source_anchor": "stmt-p-finite-memory-tradeoff",
      "source_pm_id": "PM-140.4",
      "source_quote": "every nonnegative integer $M$, and every $0<\\epsilon\\le1/10$, a learner in the experiment above with uniform-sphere average success at least $2/3$",
      "target_anchor": "h:main",
      "target_pm_id": "PM-140.1",
      "target_quote": "let $0\\le M\\le Ad^2$ be an integer, and let $0<\\epsilon\\le1/10$.",
      "verification": "PENDING"
    },
    {
      "basis": "inferred",
      "direction": "directed",
      "explanation": "The sample lower bound generalises the average case streaming bound by establishing that the sample complexity lower bound also holds pointwise for every unit direction on the sphere.",
      "relation": "generalises",
      "source_anchor": "stmt-p-regression",
      "source_pm_id": "PM-140.4",
      "source_quote": "The dimension threshold may depend on the memory sequence. In particular, the conclusion holds when the same success probability is required separately for every $s\\in S^{d-1}$.",
      "target_anchor": "thm:streaming",
      "target_pm_id": "PM-140.2",
      "target_quote": "The eventual threshold may depend on the memory sequence.",
      "verification": "PENDING"
    },
    {
      "basis": "inferred",
      "direction": "directed",
      "explanation": "The regression lower bound generalises the precision lower bound from learners satisfying a pointwise guarantee for every signal direction to learners satisfying an average case guarantee under the uniform prior.",
      "relation": "generalises",
      "source_anchor": "stmt-p-regression",
      "source_pm_id": "PM-140.4",
      "source_quote": "every learner in the experiment above whose uniform-sphere average success satisfies",

      "target_pm_id": "PM-140.3",

      "verification": "PENDING"
    },
